* France’s Wildfires Are Swallowing Its Politics
  Foreign Policy | 31 Jul 20:59 UTC
  The country’s next presidential election is more ominous in the glow of its latest environmental disaster.
  FULL TEXT: On Friday, July 24, firefighters battling the unprecedented forest fires in southwestern France glimpsed an equally unprecedented phenomenon: a pyrocumulonimbus , better known as a fire cloud. Already common in the western United States and Canada, fire clouds are a storm within a storm, wrought by extraordinary amounts of heat and energy that belch great plumes of fire that spawn fiery tornadoes that carbonize everything and everyone in their paths. Yet a different kind of pyrocumulonimbus, no less unprecedented, has been wreaking havoc on the country’s political landscape. It is a phenomenon where the intensifying friction between the ideological extremes at both ends will create an inferno that will consume the parties sandwiched in between—and perhaps even volatize the foundations of the Fifth Republic. On Friday, July 24, firefighters battling the unprecedented forest fires in southwestern France glimpsed an equally unprecedented phenomenon: a pyrocumulonimbus , better known as a fire cloud. Already common in the western United States and Canada, fire clouds are a storm within a storm, wrought by extraordinary amounts of heat and energy that belch great plumes of fire that spawn fiery tornadoes that carbonize everything and everyone in their paths. Yet a different kind of pyrocumulonimbus, no less unprecedented, has been wreaking havoc on the country’s political landscape. It is a phenomenon where the intensifying friction between the ideological extremes at both ends will create an inferno that will consume the parties sandwiched in between—and perhaps even volatize the foundations of the Fifth Republic. The Fifth Republic, the political home that Charles de Gaulle built for France in 1958, had always been defined as a two-bloc system. The meteoric rise to the presidency of Emmanuel Macron, running as an independent who founded his own party, Republic on the Move, fatefully splintered that tradition—and the snap legislative elections that he called for in 2024 shattered it entirely. When the results were tallied, there were not two, but three blocs : an extreme-right bloc supercharged by the stunning success of Marine Le Pen’s National Front (RN); an unstable (and eventually dissolved) left-wing coalition dominated by Jean-Luc Mélenchon’s extreme-left France Unbowed (LFI); and the scorched remains of Macron’s party, which spectacularly failed to live up to its new name, Renaissance. Since then, the country has witnessed the flailing efforts of its government to pass bills and the fecklessness of a president more attentive to his legacy than to his fellow citizens. With less than eight months from the presidential election, scheduled for April 2027, the firestorms galloping across western France have now cast a macabre glow on the political paralysis in Paris. During the month of July, while the fires consumed homes, farms, and businesses in the Gironde, the political class in Paris has been consumed in a bonfire of vanities, pinning blame on one another rather than preparing for a near future that, due to climate change, promises more and worse of the same. Everyone who is politically someone agrees that France’s human and material resources for firefighting have proved to be dramatically inadequate to battle the infernos. What they vociferously disagree on, however, is whether this lack of material was inevitable. The most hotly debated issue has been over the country’s aging fleet of amphibious firefighting planes, the Canadairs. Though a dozen planes comprise the fleet, five have been out of service since the start of the fires, nearly halving its effectiveness. Inevitably, figures from across the political spectrum have joined in a chorus of criticism, faulting Macron’s government for this shoddy state of affairs. Yet the truth is more complicated. Having made the environment a centerpiece of both of his presidential campaigns, Macron’s political opponents have turned his words into a club to beat him, underscoring t
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/07/31/france-wildfires-macron-politics-le-pen-melenchon/

At least 57 people died, including from drowning or stampedes. “It’s a situation of absolute humanitarian and social emergency,” Vivas told local media , urging Madrid to declare a national emergency in Ceuta. Morocco has also urged its citizens to return home. The incident has sparked fierce outrage from EU lawmakers who have accused Spain of being an outlier in European immigration policy. Unlike many EU countries, whose rising far-right parties have cracked down on migration, left-leaning Sánchez has taken a different approach. This year, Madrid announced a law allowing more than 1 million undocumented migrants in the country to apply to formalize their status. * What Homer’s ‘Odyssey’ Tells Us About the Economics of the Bronze Age
  Foreign Policy | 31 Jul 19:30 UTC
  The epic poem describes events around the sacking of Troy.
  FULL TEXT: Prefer to listen? To hear this entire conversation, and more episodes in the weeks ahead, follow Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts. As one of the most popular works of literature of all time, as well as the subject of the current most popular movie in the world, the Odyssey has long been analyzed from a political perspective. But the classic adventure tale also offers important insights into the economics of ancient Greece—and of today. What does the Greek etymology of the word “economy” reveal about its meaning? How does Greek theology inform ideas of economic risk? Why has Odysseus’s relationship to the sirens been used as metaphor to explain the work of central banks? As one of the most popular works of literature of all time, as well as the subject of the current most popular movie in the world, the Odyssey has long been analyzed from a political perspective. But the classic adventure tale also offers important insights into the economics of ancient Greece—and of today. What does the Greek etymology of the word “economy” reveal about its meaning? How does Greek theology inform ideas of economic risk? Why has Odysseus’s relationship to the sirens been used as metaphor to explain the work of central banks? Those are just a few of the questions that came up in my recent conversation with FP economics columnist Adam Tooze on the podcast we co-host, Ones and Tooze . What follows is an excerpt, edited for length and clarity. For the full conversation, look for Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts. And check out Adam’s Substack newsletter. Cameron Abadi: What kind of economic history about the Trojan War is relevant as background for the Odyssey ? Adam Tooze: The crucial thing to realize is that the Homeric epics are from about 800 B.C., which is 400 years before classical Greece, Athens, Sparta, and so on. And they are about events which are 400 years before Homer. So, they’re about a period that we would roughly place at about 1200 B.C. So this is as though we were, in a complicated, arcane, poetic way, writing poetry in 2020 about stuff that happened under Charlemagne. The date commonly given for the sack of Troy is something like 1250 [B.C.]. And what that does is to place this within the frame of what is now commonly referred to as the Bronze Age, which is a period in the eastern Mediterranean of great imperial, large-scale state structures. Egypt’s still there from much, much earlier. The Hittites in central Turkey, the Greeks organized in the Mycenaean state structures and statelets, the Babylonians, and many more. And the key commodity is bronze, which itself is an amalgam of copper and tin. And so the great-power economics of the age are thought to have revolved essentially around the trade in those two commodities, of which copper was relatively abundantly available, whereas tin had to come from as far away as Afghanistan by ship. And that’s what we think the economy of Troy was most likely based on, which was basically a kind of toll-taking on shipments of tin to the Mycenaean Greek states. And from the early 1200s through to the mid-1100s B.C., a total catastrophe hits the eastern Mediterranean civilizations. It’s known as the great crisis of the Bronze Age, out of which only Egypt survived. So all of the other state structures were massively disrupted. And so, Homer’s epic is looking back not just at the disaster of the war, but also at a civilization that is on the cusp of disintegration. And we think it’s an effect of warmaking, various types of dysfunctional economic structure, and of climate change that by the 1100s already had essentially destroyed all of the major civilizations of this era. So it’s not for nothing in Western civilization, in Western culture that this epic marks the kind of foundational shock of Western civilization and its epics and its telling of these stories.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/07/31/christopher-nolan-homer-odyssey-economics-bronze-age-adam-tooze/

Half a century later, however, it turns out it was about Pete Hegseth all along. On the Psychology of Military Incompetence , by British psychologist Norman F. Dixon, has long been considered a must-read, even within U.S. military circles that relentlessly cull their own reading lists. Dixon sought to answer a question that had long bedeviled the British military, most especially in the wake of World War I: Why are lions always seemingly led by donkeys ? This year marks the 50th anniversary of the publication of a very British book seeking to make sense of what seemed at the time a very British problem. Half a century later, however, it turns out it was about Pete Hegseth all along. On the Psychology of Military Incompetence , by British psychologist Norman F. Dixon, has long been considered a must-read, even within U.S. military circles that relentlessly cull their own reading lists. Dixon sought to answer a question that had long bedeviled the British military, most especially in the wake of World War I: Why are lions always seemingly led by donkeys ? Dixon had plenty of material to work with for his case studies, which make up the first part of the book. The series of military disasters that befell the British Army—and even occasionally the Royal Navy—began with a pair of catastrophic 19th century forays into Afghanistan, continued with a ham-fisted nightmare in the Crimean War, somehow got worse in an unequal battle with the Boers, and then culminated in the bloody mud flats of Passchendaele. World War II had its awful moments as well, especially at the beginning (the surrender of Singapore in early 1942 while heavily outnumbering the Japanese invasion forces remains Britain’s worst defeat) but also near the end, when the British Army attempted a paradrop push combined with an armored thrust into the Netherlands that proved to be a “bridge too far.” On the Psychology of Military Incompetence , Norman F. Dixon, Basic Books, 528 pp., $22.99, first ed. 1976 Dixon’s book reaches back to the middle of the 19th century and is thoroughly grounded in antiquated British military and civilian mores (Dixon places much of the blame on Victorian public school traumas and codes of behavior) and somewhat archaic psychology. There’s a lot on mommy issues and other Freudian cliches of the time. Yet, it is impossible to read the book today without thinking of the self-proclaimed U.S. “secretary of war” and his hapless efforts to root out “ beardos ,” do a pull-up , or subdue a third-rate country with history’s most awesome military at his disposal. Dixon rules out pure stupidity as a root cause for most of the explanation of the continued incompetence. Many of his case studies focus on general officers who were either academic high achievers, such as Douglas Haig, the commander of British forces for most of World War I, or at least reasonably free from intellectual defects. Instead, Dixon, who spent 10 years in the army, including service as a combat engineer in World War II, seeks an answer in deeper waters: a particular mindset attracted to institutions such as the military, which then exacerbate by design the very tendencies that will tend to undermine military competence. By and large, the (British) military shunned innovation; prized physical prowess and sporting achievements over book smarts (“lethal warrior” mentality); cherished tradition, spit-and-polish, regulations, and petty rules that all come under what Dixon defines as “bullshit.” Soldiers that had those traits to a great degree naturally advanced to higher ranks, eventually the highest, precisely to positions where those characteristics were either useless or catastrophic. Another characteristic Dixon found in decades of military dysfunction was an ingrained fear of “effeminacy,” pro
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/07/31/hegseth-norman-dixon-iran-war/

Virginia struck down laws banning interracial marriage. * Resurrecting the Real Monroe Doctrine
  Foreign Policy | 31 Jul 18:20 UTC
  Hidden behind two centuries of aggressive presidential corollaries is an enduring appeal to hemispheric solidarity.
  FULL TEXT: The 250th anniversary of U.S. independence has revived a debate on what it means to be American. This has spotlighted at least two competing stories on American identity: One hinges on a conventional understanding of national identity as a state-centered project characterized by jurisdictional boundaries and physical separation between peoples; a competing story, based on shared experiences, cultures, and diasporas, claims that “American” is a term that cuts across national differences and refers to the entire Western Hemisphere. Both accounts of American culture and identity have been taken up recently in public discourse. U.S. President Donald Trump’s administration has made the go-it-alone, state-centered narrative a central dimension of its historical nostalgia. As the White House states , the goal of its 250th anniversary programming is to “inspire a renewed love for American history.” In response, popular discourse has embraced a hemispheric conception of American culture that celebrates diasporic traditions while critiquing the imperial tendencies of the United States. This framing was famously deployed by Bad Bunny during the Super Bowl LX halftime show , but it has also been used to think about language , history , and artistic production . This has spotlighted at least two competing stories on American identity: One hinges on a conventional understanding of national identity as a state-centered project characterized by jurisdictional boundaries and physical separation between peoples; a competing story, based on shared experiences, cultures, and diasporas, claims that “American” is a term that cuts across national differences and refers to the entire Western Hemisphere. Both accounts of American culture and identity have been taken up recently in public discourse. U.S. President Donald Trump’s administration has made the go-it-alone, state-centered narrative a central dimension of its historical nostalgia. As the White House states , the goal of its 250th anniversary programming is to “inspire a renewed love for American history.” In response, popular discourse has embraced a hemispheric conception of American culture that celebrates diasporic traditions while critiquing the imperial tendencies of the United States. This framing was famously deployed by Bad Bunny during the Super Bowl LX halftime show , but it has also been used to think about language , history , and artistic production . Recent scholarship on the so-called age of revolutions—the period from around 1775 to 1840 characterized by popular uprisings against colonial rule—has supported this latter definition, as it has uncovered shared concerns , bonds , and political innovations throughout the Western Hemisphere. In my new book, A New World of Revolutions , I argue for this approach as well, situating the U.S. revolutionary tradition among the publics, identities, and cultures that spanned from the Northern Arctic to Patagonia. These histories reinforce the notion that American identities and ideas far exceed the institutional and physical boundaries of the nation-state. Despite evidence of these hemispheric bonds, Trump likes to return to this period to justify U.S. intervention across the world. Celebratory programming around the 250th anniversary explicitly connected his America First approach to the Monroe Doctrine , a 19th-century foreign-policy principle that Trump’s White House interprets as a declaration that Washington should lead the hemisphere and guard it “against communism, fascism, and foreign infringement.” Yet, while the Trump administration clearly admires the Monroe Doctrine, it does not appear to understand the hemispheric politics, histories, and identitie

* The Myth of African Heterosexual Purity

In early March, Senegalese lawmaker Diaraye Ba declared before the legislature, “Homosexuals will no longer breathe in this country; homosexuals will no longer have freedom of expression in this country.” Chillingly, she was met with applause. Pure Men: A Novel , Mohamed Mbougar Sarr, Other Press, 192 pp., $10.99, June 2026 Senegal’s president was about to approve a bill raising the maximum prison term for same-sex relations from five to 10 years and criminalizing advocacy for LGBTQ+ rights. The ban’s language casts queerness as a threat to the health and survival of Senegalese people in a country where queerness is largely viewed as a Western import that corrupts the values of the Muslim majority, and gay men are seen as nefarious spreaders of HIV. Senegalese writer Mohamed Mbougar Sarr’s novel, Pure Men , shines a keen light on this ongoing nightmare. First published in 2018, the book, now translated from the French by Lara Vergnaud, tells the story of Ndene Gueye, a Dakarian professor of literature whose encounters with homophobic violence force him to reckon with what one character describes as Senegal’s lethal moral “totalitarianism.” An explosive cross between a social thriller and a novel of ideas, Sarr’s book probes the roots of Senegalese homophobia, exploring how religious ideals combine with the myth of African heterosexual purity to sustain a culture of murderous hate. It also identifies a fundamental challenge facing those who resist Senegal’s systemic homophobia: how to articulate a queer politics of liberation while refraining from a simplistic characterization of Islam as a religion at odds with Western modernity. Members of Senegal’s legislature celebrate after the adoption of a stricter law prohibiting homosexuality in March. Seyllou/AFP via Getty Images Pure Men is preoccupied less with homophobic microaggression and ridicule than with the radical, irrational, and bloodthirsty forms of anti-queer prejudice that have evolved in Senegal since 2008, when a local magazine published pictures of a gay marriage and sparked national outrage. The novel opens on Ndene viewing a viral video of a supposed goor-jigeen (the Wolof slur for queer, which translates literally to “man-woman”)—being exhumed by a mob from his grave in a Muslim cemetery. In Islam, those who engage in same-sex acts are believed to have sinned, and, according to Senegalese Muslims, thus cannot be buried among other Muslims. Ndene’s initial reaction to the video is callous, and he impulsively dismisses the man as “just a góor-jigéen .” But Ndene then experiences a moment of crisis. Had he spoken from personal conviction, or through a “shared mouth, a mass grave of sorts in which were buried—albeit resuscitated often—the general opinions of a country”? Befuddled, Ndene teeters toward a grim realization: “I was the mouth of ancient forces that had the powers of life and death over me.” These complicated emotions trigger a quest for knowledge. Ndene determines to find out more about the man in the video: “Who had accused him? Had there been proof of his sexual deviancy? Where was his family? What had become of his body?” The book stages Ndene’s reckoning with his homophobic milieu as a series of epiphanies, the first of which is his recognition of the moral and ontological depth of the s
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/07/31/pure-men-senegal-mohamed-sarr-homophobia-queer-africa-islam-religion/
